Assess this manuscript's publication potential honestly. You are not its author and you gain nothing from flattering it.

Read main.tex and its section files, the artifacts/ verification scripts and their output, and any oracle_sprint_*.md replies in artifacts/ (those are an external expert's rounds, already filtered by a verifier).

Answer these, in order, in ENGLISH, concisely:

1. HEADLINE RESULT. State the single strongest theorem in one sentence, as a mathematician would state it. Give its label.

2. IS IT ACTUALLY NEW? Judge independently. Search arXiv/MathSciNet-style knowledge for the nearest prior work and name it. Then classify the headline as one of:
   - GENUINELY NEW (no prior work does this)
   - INCREMENTAL (a known result extended by a step)
   - REPACKAGING (known content in new notation)
   Justify in two sentences with the specific prior work.

3. DEPTH OF THE MATHEMATICS. Is the proof technique substantial (new mechanism, hard estimate, genuine obstruction overcome), or is it bookkeeping/computation? Be blunt.

4. TOP-JOURNAL VERDICT. Could this, as it stands, plausibly be accepted at a top-tier journal in its field (Annals/Inventiones/JAMS tier = TIER-1; JFA/ETDS/Trans.AMS/Math.Comp tier = TIER-2; solid specialist journal = TIER-3; borderline/niche = TIER-4)? Give ONE tier and one sentence of justification. Do not inflate.

5. WHAT WOULD MOVE IT UP ONE TIER. The single most valuable missing theorem or strengthening, stated precisely. If nothing realistic would move it up, say so.

6. WEAKEST LINK. The claim most likely to draw a referee's objection, and why.

Be concise: at most 30 lines total. No praise, no hedging filler.

## Instruction Priority

Superpowers skills override default system prompt behavior, but **user instructions always take precedence**:

1. **User's explicit instructions** (CLAUDE.md, GEMINI.md, AGENTS.md, direct requests) â€” highest priority
2. **Superpowers skills** â€” override default system behavior where they conflict
3. **Default system prompt** â€” lowest priority

If CLAUDE.md, GEMINI.md, or AGENTS.md says "don't use TDD" and a skill says "always use TDD," follow the user's instructions. The user is in control.

## How to Access Skills

**In Claude Code:** Use the `Skill` tool. When you invoke a skill, its content is loaded and presented to youâ€”follow it directly. Never use the Read tool on skill files.

**In Copilot CLI:** Use the `skill` tool. Skills are auto-discovered from installed plugins. The `skill` tool works the same as Claude Code's `Skill` tool.

**In Gemini CLI:** Skills activate via the `activate_skill` tool. Gemini loads skill metadata at session start and activates the full content on demand.

**In other environments:** Check your platform's documentation for how skills are loaded.

## Platform Adaptation

Skills use Claude Code tool names. Non-CC platforms: see `references/copilot-tools.md` (Copilot CLI), `references/codex-tools.md` (Codex) for tool equivalents. Gemini CLI users get the tool mapping loaded automatically via GEMINI.md.

# Using Skills

## The Rule

**Invoke relevant or requested skills BEFORE any response or action.** Even a 1% chance a skill might apply means that you should invoke the skill to check. If an invoked skill turns out to be wrong for the situation, you don't need to use it.

```dot
digraph skill_flow {
    "User message received" [shape=doublecircle];
    "About to EnterPlanMode?" [shape=doublecircle];
    "Already brainstormed?" [shape=diamond];
    "Invoke brainstorming skill" [shape=box];
    "Might any skill apply?" [shape=diamond];
    "Invoke Skill tool" [shape=box];
    "Announce: 'Using [skill] to [purpose]'" [shape=box];
    "Has checklist?" [shape=diamond];
    "Create TodoWrite todo per item" [shape=box];
    "Follow skill exactly" [shape=box];
    "Respond (including clarifications)" [shape=doublecircle];

    "About to EnterPlanMode?" -> "Already brainstormed?";
    "Already brainstormed?" -> "Invoke brainstorming skill" [label="no"];
    "Already brainstormed?" -> "Might any skill apply?" [label="yes"];
    "Invoke brainstorming skill" -> "Might any skill apply?";

    "User message received" -> "Might any skill apply?";
    "Might any skill apply?" -> "Invoke Skill tool" [label="yes, even 1%"];
    "Might any skill apply?" -> "Respond (including clarifications)" [label="definitely not"];
    "Invoke Skill tool" -> "Announce: 'Using [skill] to [purpose]'";
    "Announce: 'Using [skill] to [purpose]'" -> "Has checklist?";
    "Has checklist?" -> "Create TodoWrite todo per item" [label="yes"];
    "Has checklist?" -> "Follow skill exactly" [label="no"];
    "Create TodoWrite todo per item" -> "Follow skill exactly";
}
```

## Red Flags

These thoughts mean STOPâ€”you're rationalizing:

| Thought | Reality |
|---------|---------|
| "This is just a simple question" | Questions are tasks. Check for skills. |
| "I need more context first" | Skill check comes BEFORE clarifying questions. |
| "Let me explore the codebase first" | Skills tell you HOW to explore. Check first. |
| "I can check git/files quickly" | Files lack conversation context. Check for skills. |
| "Let me gather information first" | Skills tell you HOW to gather information. |
| "This doesn't need a formal skill" | If a skill exists, use it. |
| "I remember this skill" | Skills evolve. Read current version. |
| "This doesn't count as a task" | Action = task. Check for skills. |
| "The skill is overkill" | Simple things become complex. Use it. |
| "I'll just do this one thing first" | Check BEFORE doing anything. |
| "This feels productive" | Undisciplined action wastes time. Skills prevent this. |
| "I know what that means" | Knowing the concept â‰  using the skill. Invoke it. |

## Skill Priority

When multiple skills could apply, use this order:

1. **Process skills first** (brainstorming, debugging) - these determine HOW to approach the task
2. **Implementation skills second** (frontend-design, mcp-builder) - these guide execution

"Let's build X" â†’ brainstorming first, then implementation skills.
"Fix this bug" â†’ debugging first, then domain-specific skills.

## Skill Types

**Rigid** (TDD, debugging): Follow exactly. Don't adapt away discipline.

**Flexible** (patterns): Adapt principles to context.

The skill itself tells you which.

## User Instructions

Instructions say WHAT, not HOW. "Add X" or "Fix Y" doesn't mean skip workflows.

\title[Finite-window Zeckendorf fibers]{%
  Finite-Window Zeckendorf Fibers:\\
  Affine Partition Correspondence and Negative-Temperature Freezing}

\author{Haobo Ma}
\address{AELF PTE LTD., \#14-02, Marina Bay Financial Centre Tower 1,
8 Marina Blvd, Singapore 018981, Singapore}
\email{auric@aelf.io}

\author{Wenlin Zhang}
\address{National University of Singapore, Singapore}
\email{e1327962@u.nus.edu}
\hypersetup{
  pdftitle={Finite-Window Zeckendorf Fibers: Affine Partition Correspondence and Negative-Temperature Freezing},
  pdfauthor={Haobo Ma and Wenlin Zhang},
  pdfsubject={Finite-window fibers of Fibonacci subset sums},
  pdfkeywords={Zeckendorf representation, Fibonacci partition function, extremal fibers, power sums, pressure, negative-temperature freezing}
}
\date{}
\subjclass[2020]{11B39, 05A17, 60F10}
\keywords{Zeckendorf representation, Fibonacci partition function, extremal
fibers, power sums, pressure, negative-temperature freezing}

\begin{document}

\begin{abstract}
We study the fibers of a finite-window map that sends a binary Fibonacci sum
to the legal word representing its residue modulo the next Fibonacci number.
An explicit affine permutation identifies the fiber multiplicities with the
coefficients of a finite Fibonacci subset-sum polynomial and, pointwise, with
a Fibonacci-lag difference of the classical partition function.  A second
identity identifies the complete fiber list with values of that partition
function on two consecutive intervals.  The known extremal classification on
these intervals then gives
\(D_{2k}=F_{k+2}\), \(D_{2k+1}=2F_{k+1}\), and
\(D_m=D_{m-2}+D_{m-4}\) for \(m\ge6\), together with all maximizing
residues.  For integer \(q\ge1\), the fiber power sums have the same
exponential constants as the partition power sums studied by Sanna; in the
quadratic case we give a direct cubic recurrence.  For real negative tilts we
prove a Dirichlet-series freezing bound.  If \(\sigma_0>2\) is determined by
\(\zeta(\sigma_0-1)/\zeta(\sigma_0)=2\), then
\(\lim_{m\to\infty}m^{-1}\log\sum_x d_m(x)^t=0\) for every
\(t\le-\sigma_0\).  The endpoint proof uses an explicit transfer of
Weinstein's orbit formulas, including the exceptional levels and early
layers.  At the finer, unnormalized scale, the fixed multiplicity counts
stabilize and the negative-tilt sums converge exactly to
\(4(2-\zeta(s-1)/\zeta(s))^{-1}-2\) for \(s>\sigma_0\), with a simple pole
at \(\sigma_0\) and a total-variation Gibbs limit.  A Wiener--Ikehara
argument gives the associated generating-orbit asymptotic.  A weighted renewal
identity for Weinstein's free generators, combined with the published
Stern--Brocot denominator pressure, proves that the pressure exists for every
real tilt.  It is real analytic and strictly convex on
\((-\sigma_0,\infty)\), but has a first-order corner at \(-\sigma_0\).
An orbit-filling construction closes the nonexposed slopes at this corner:
the uniform log-fiber thickness satisfies a full large deviation principle,
whose rate is affine on the coexistence interval.
Conditioning on a fixed microcanonical band inside that interval produces a
sharper phase separation: the log multiplicity concentrates at the upper band
edge and the generating-layer proportion converges to that edge divided by
the critical velocity.  The conclusion persists for shrinking bands with
positive limiting lattice width, while a family of heavy generator letters
gives a polynomial conditional upper tail.
At exact dyadic multiplicities, an arithmetic renewal law gives the quenched
generator-cost velocity and its finite-window transfer under a positive layer
margin.  An explicit interval certificate proves that this dyadic velocity is
strictly larger than the critical annealed velocity, furnishing realizable
exact multiplier sequences on which the critical band speed fails.
The frozen half-line rules out a globally analytic compact-operator
representation under precise spectral hypotheses.
\end{abstract}

prove a Dirichlet-series freezing bound.  If \(\sigma_0>2\) is determined by
\(\zeta(\sigma_0-1)/\zeta(\sigma_0)=2\), then
\(\lim_{m\to\infty}m^{-1}\log\sum_x d_m(x)^t=0\) for every
\(t\le-\sigma_0\).  The endpoint proof uses an explicit transfer of
Weinstein's orbit formulas, including the exceptional levels and early
layers.  At the finer, unnormalized scale, the fixed multiplicity counts
stabilize and the negative-tilt sums converge exactly to
\(4(2-\zeta(s-1)/\zeta(s))^{-1}-2\) for \(s>\sigma_0\), with a simple pole
at \(\sigma_0\) and a total-variation Gibbs limit.  A Wiener--Ikehara
argument gives the associated generating-orbit asymptotic.  A weighted renewal
identity for Weinstein's free generators, combined with the published
Stern--Brocot denominator pressure, proves that the pressure exists for every
real tilt.  It is real analytic and strictly convex on
\((-\sigma_0,\infty)\), but has a first-order corner at \(-\sigma_0\).
An orbit-filling construction closes the nonexposed slopes at this corner:
the uniform log-fiber thickness satisfies a full large deviation principle,
whose rate is affine on the coexistence interval.
Conditioning on a fixed microcanonical band inside that interval produces a
sharper phase separation: the log multiplicity concentrates at the upper band
edge and the generating-layer proportion converges to that edge divided by
the critical velocity.  The conclusion persists for shrinking bands with
positive limiting lattice width, while a family of heavy generator letters
gives a polynomial conditional upper tail.
At exact dyadic multiplicities, an arithmetic renewal law gives the quenched
generator-cost velocity and its finite-window transfer under a positive layer
margin.  An explicit interval certificate proves that this dyadic velocity is
strictly larger than the critical annealed velocity, furnishing realizable
exact multiplier sequences on which the critical band speed fails.
The frozen half-line rules out a globally analytic compact-operator
representation under precise spectral hypotheses.
\end{abstract}

\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}

Let \(F_0=0\), \(F_1=F_2=1\), and
\(F_{j+2}=F_{j+1}+F_j\).  A word
\(\omega\in\{0,1\}^m\) determines the raw sum
\[
  N_m(\omega)=\sum_{j=1}^m\omega_jF_{j+1}.
\]
We consider the finite-window fold obtained by reducing this sum modulo
\(F_{m+2}\) and taking the unique legal Zeckendorf word in the visible
window.  If \(d_m(x)\) is the cardinality of the fiber over a legal word
\(x\), the basic problem is to identify the finite multiset
\(\{d_m(x):x\in X_m\}\) and its extremal and moment statistics.

The paper is centered on two exact correspondences with the classical
Fibonacci partition function.  The first is an affine reindexing of a finite
subset-sum spectrum; the second expresses the complete fiber list as two
consecutive blocks of ordinary partition values.  These correspondences are
finite identities and do not require a normalization transducer.

\subsection{Main results}

Let \(R(n)\) be the number of partitions of \(n\) into distinct Fibonacci
numbers when the two unit weights are not distinguished, with \(R(n)=0\) for
\(n<0\), and put \(R^\dagger(n)=R(n)+R(n-1)\).  Let
\[
  \widetilde d_m(n)=[z^n]\prod_{j=1}^m(1+z^{F_j}),
  \qquad 0\le n<F_{m+2}.
\]
For residues modulo \(F_{m+2}\), define
\[
  \pi_m(n)\equiv F_{m+1}n+F_{m+1}-1\pmod{F_{m+2}}.
\]

\begin{theoremA}[Affine fiber--partition correspondence]
For every \(m\ge1\), the map \(\pi_m\) is a permutation and
\[
  d_m^\#(\pi_m(n))
  =\widetilde d_m(n)
  =R^\dagger(n)-R^\dagger(n-F_{m+1})
  \qquad(0\le n<F_{m+2}).
\]
\end{theoremA}

This combines \Cref{thm:affine-transfer,thm:partition-difference}.  It gives a
pointwise correspondence, not only an equality of aggregate moments.

There is also a direct interval description:
\[
 d_m^\#(r)=
 \begin{cases}
 R(F_{m+2}+r),&0\le r\le F_{m+1}-2,\\
 R(r),&F_{m+1}-1\le r<F_{m+2}.
 \end{cases}
\]
The known extremal classification of Koc\'abov\'a, Mas\'akov\'a, and
Pelantov\'a can therefore be transferred exactly after an explicit change of
Fibonacci convention.

\begin{theoremB}[Interval identity and largest fibers]
Let \(D_m=\max_{x\in X_m}d_m(x)\).  Then
\[
 D_0=D_1=1,\qquad D_{2k}=F_{k+2}\ (k\ge0),\qquad
 D_{2k+1}=2F_{k+1}\ (k\ge1),
\]
and \(D_m=D_{m-2}+D_{m-4}\) for every \(m\ge6\).  The maximizing residues,
including the exceptional initial cases, are given in
\Cref{thm:unconditional-extremal-fibers}.
\end{theoremB}

For a real tilt \(t\), put
\[
  S_t(m)=\sum_{x\in X_m}d_m(x)^t.
\]
The level sets of \(R\) are sufficiently sparse to produce a frozen
half-line.  Let \(\sigma_0>2\) be the unique solution of
\[
  \frac{\zeta(\sigma_0-1)}{\zeta(\sigma_0)}=2;
  \qquad \sigma_0=2.4787507857\ldots.
\]

\begin{theoremC}[Negative-temperature freezing]
For every real \(t\le-\sigma_0\),
\[
  \lim_{m\to\infty}\frac1m\log S_t(m)=0.
\]
Consequently these pressure limits cannot be represented on all real tilts by
the logarithm of a positive simple isolated eigenvalue of an analytic compact
operator family on a fixed Banach space.
\end{theoremC}

The freezing theorem certifies the half-line \(t\le-\sigma_0\).  The weighted
renewal theorem below supplies the complementary positivity in the candidate
strip and proves that \(-\sigma_0\) is the exact zero-pressure boundary.

The fixed-level information is sharper than the exponential pressure.  Put
\(C_m(k)=\card{\{x\in X_m:d_m(x)=k\}}\), and let \(\Psi(k)\) be Weinstein's
number of generating orbits at level \(k\).

\begin{theoremD}[Local spectrum and critical Gibbs law]
For \(m\ge1\), \(C_m(1)=2\), while for \(k\ge2\),
\[
 C_m(k)\le4\Psi(k),
 \qquad C_m(k)=4\Psi(k)\quad(m\ge2k).
\]
For every \(s>\sigma_0\),
\[
 S_{-s}(m)\longrightarrow
 \mathcal Z(s):=\frac4{2-\zeta(s-1)/\zeta(s)}-2.
\]
This is the exact convergence range for the unnormalized sums.  The function
\(\mathcal Z\) has a simple pole at \(\sigma_0\), and the corresponding
fiber-level Gibbs measures converge in total variation with explicit
critical logarithmic moments.
\end{theoremD}

The complete statements are
\Cref{thm:local-fiber-spectrum,thm:spectral-partition-function,thm:critical-gibbs-spectrum}.
They separate an unnormalized critical phenomenon inside the region where
the exponential pressure vanishes.

\begin{theoremE}[Orbit growth and all-real pressure]
With
\[
 \kappa=-\left.\frac{d}{ds}\frac{\zeta(s-1)}{\zeta(s)}
 \right|_{s=\sigma_0},
\]
one has
\[
 \sum_{k\le x}\Psi(k)\sim
 \frac{x^{\sigma_0}}{\sigma_0\kappa}.
\]
Moreover, if
\(\beta_*=\log\varphi/\log(2/\varphi)=2.2705594539\ldots\), then
\[
 \liminf_{m\to\infty}\frac1m\log S_t(m)>0
 \qquad(t>-\beta_*).
\]
For every real \(t\), the limit \(P(t)\) exists.  If
\(t>-\sigma_0\), it is positive and is determined by the
weighted-generator equation
\[
 \sum_{q\ge2}\sum_{\substack{1\le p<q\\(p,q)=1}}
 q^t e^{-P(t)c(p/q)}=1.
\]
For \(t\le-\sigma_0\), one has \(P(t)=0\).  On
\((-\sigma_0,\infty)\), the pressure is real analytic and strictly convex.
At \(t_c=-\sigma_0\), its left derivative is zero and its right derivative is
a finite, explicitly convergent positive ratio of continued-fraction moments.
\end{theoremE}

The Tauberian statement invokes the classical Wiener--Ikehara theorem; the
new input is the pole and boundary analysis of Weinstein's generating-orbit
Dirichlet series.  The all-real pressure statement follows from
\Cref{prop:weighted-generator-renewal,thm:all-real-pressure}; its published
Stern--Brocot/Knauf input and its new finite-window consequences are separated
explicitly there.

Let \(\mathbf X_m\) be uniformly distributed on \(X_m\), and put
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 Y_m=m^{-1}\log d_m(\mathbf X_m),\\
 t_c=-\sigma_0,\qquad v_c=P'_+(t_c),\qquad
 \alpha_+=\frac12\log\varphi.
 \end{gathered}
\]

\begin{theoremF}[Full large deviations across the freezing corner]
The sequence \((Y_m)\) satisfies a large deviation principle with speed
\(m\) and good rate function
\[
 I(\alpha)=\sup_{t\in\RR}\{t\alpha-P(t)+\log\varphi\}.
\]
Its effective domain is \([0,\alpha_+]\), and
\[
 I(\alpha)=\log\varphi-\sigma_0\alpha
 \qquad(0\le\alpha\le v_c).
\]
For every \(0<\alpha<v_c\), the probabilities of shrinking neighborhoods
have the exact exponential rate \(-I(\alpha)\).
\end{theoremF}

The complete statement and proof are given in \Cref{thm:full-fiber-ldp}.
The new orbit-filling step fills both parities of every sufficiently large
target layer while preserving the source multiplicity.

For \(0<\alpha<v_c\) and
\(0<\varepsilon<\min\{\alpha,v_c-\alpha\}\), let
\(\mathbf X_{m,\alpha,\varepsilon}\) be uniform on the states satisfying
\(|m^{-1}\log d_m-\alpha|<\varepsilon\).  When the multiplicity exceeds two,
write \(g_m\) for the unique Weinstein generator of the corresponding orbit
and \(L(g_m)\) for its layer cost.

\begin{theoremG}[Finite-window conditional phase separation]
One has
\[
 \left(m^{-1}\log d_m(\mathbf X_{m,\alpha,\varepsilon}),
       m^{-1}L(g_m(\mathbf X_{m,\alpha,\varepsilon}))\right)
 \xrightarrow{\Pr}
 \left(\alpha+\varepsilon,
       \frac{\alpha+\varepsilon}{v_c}\right).
\]
Hence the iterated small-window limit of the second coordinate is
\(\alpha/v_c\).  The same conclusion holds for
\(\varepsilon_m\downarrow0\) whenever
\(\liminf m\varepsilon_m>0\).  For a fixed window, however, some upper
deviation has probability at least \(cm^{1-\sigma_0}\).
\end{theoremG}

The thin-window arithmetic interface has an exact solution along the dyadic
multiplier ray.  Define \(\gamma_2\) by
\eqref{eq:dyadic-cost-velocity} and put \(v_2=\log2/\gamma_2\).

\begin{theoremH}[Exact dyadic multiplier limit]
If \(r_m\to\infty\) and \(m/r_m\to\beta>\gamma_2\), then
\[
 C_m(2^{r_m})\sim4\cdot3^{r_m-1},
 \qquad
 \frac1mL(g_m)\xrightarrow{\Pr}\frac{\gamma_2}{\beta}
\]
under the uniform law on the exact level \(d_m=2^{r_m}\).  In particular,
for \(r_m=\lfloor\alpha m/\log2\rfloor\) and \(0<\alpha<v_2\), the second
limit is \(\alpha/v_2\).
Moreover,
\[
 v_2-v_c\ge0.001171960512764161>0.
\]
Consequently, for every \(0<\alpha<v_c\), these realizable exact dyadic
levels have limiting generating-layer proportion \(\alpha/v_2\), not
\(\alpha/v_c\).
\end{theoremH}

The full proof and its refinements are given in
\Cref{thm:conditional-phase-separation,cor:shrinking-window-phase-separation,thm:dyadic-exact-multiplier,thm:strict-speed-separation,prop:conditional-polynomial-upper-tail}.
The condition on shrinking widths cannot be deleted without an arithmetic
local theorem, because thinner bands can avoid every occurring multiplicity.

The proof of \Cref{thm:negative-temperature-freezing} includes a separate
layer-count lemma transferring Weinstein's convention \(f_j=F_{j+1}\).  It
distinguishes the levels \(k=1,2\), endpoints, early layers, and the eventual
equality asserted in Weinstein's theorem.  The compact-operator conclusion is
stated precisely in \Cref{prop:no-analytic-compact-representation} and follows
from analytic perturbation theory.

Two further comparisons are useful.  First, for every positive integer
\(q\), the fiber moment \(S_q(m)\) is trapped between two adjacent Fibonacci
windows of the partition power sum.  Sanna's theorem therefore gives
\(S_q(m)\asymp_q\lambda_q^m\).  Sanna also proved
\(\lambda_q^{1/q}\to\varphi^{1/2}\); our norm squeeze is recorded only as an
alternative finite-window proof.  The additional diagonal consequence is
\[
  \frac{1}{m q_m}\log S_{q_m}(m)
  \longrightarrow\frac12\log\varphi
\]
for every sequence of positive integers \(q_m\to\infty\); the same squeeze
also applies to real tilts \(t_m\to\infty\).  Second, the quadratic moment has
the independently proved recurrence
\[
  S_2(m)=2S_2(m-1)+2S_2(m-2)-2S_2(m-3)\qquad(m\ge4).
\]

The pressure corner prevents a direct full application of the
G\"artner--Ellis theorem: its lower-bound conclusion does not cover slopes
strictly between the one-sided derivatives at \(-\sigma_0\).  The
orbit-filling argument in \Cref{thm:full-fiber-ldp} supplies those missing
local lower bounds and proves the full large deviation principle, with an
affine rate on the coexistence interval.

\subsection{Contribution boundary}

The residue characterization in \Cref{thm:residue-fibers} is a direct
restatement of the fold definition, and \Cref{thm:fourier-inversion} is the
standard character-orthogonality formula.  The finite-window contributions in
\Cref{sec:residue-affine} are the explicit reversal--complement bijection and
affine residue permutation of \Cref{thm:affine-transfer}, together with the
pointwise fiber formula in \Cref{thm:partition-difference}; the latter follows
by truncating the indexed Fibonacci partition generating function.  The
two-interval fiber identity in \Cref{thm:fiber-partition-interval} is also
specific to the finite-window fold.

The values and maximizing residues in
\Cref{thm:unconditional-extremal-fibers} are not a new extremal classification:
they are the exact convention-and-interval transfer of Theorems~4.7 and~5.3 of
Koc\'abov\'a, Mas\'akov\'a, and Pelantov\'a
\cite{KocabovaMasakovaPelantova2005};
\Cref{cor:extremal-fiber-recurrence} is then an immediate Fibonacci-recurrence
consequence.  The additional ingredient in the negative-tilt argument is the
uniform early-layer estimate proved from Weinstein's orbit formulas.  That
estimate, which strengthens eventual layer stabilization to a bound valid in
every layer, yields \Cref{thm:negative-temperature-freezing};
\Cref{prop:no-analytic-compact-representation} is its analytic-perturbation
consequence.  The local microcanonical limit and the exact unnormalized
partition function in \Cref{sec:local-spectrum} combine this finite-window
two-layer identity with Weinstein's published orbit decomposition.  The
Wiener--Ikehara theorem itself is classical \cite{Korevaar2004};
\Cref{thm:psi-tauberian} is its application to the specific orbit Dirichlet
series, and \Cref{thm:pressure-transition-strip} is a separate finite-window
Jensen and maximum-level estimate.  Weinstein's free-monoid bijection and
largest-index formula, and the Stern--Brocot/Knauf denominator pressure, are
published.  The weighted length renewal in
\Cref{prop:weighted-generator-renewal}, its exact finite-window transfer, and
the all-real finite-window pressure consequence
\Cref{thm:all-real-pressure} are the additional steps here.
The standard large-deviation upper bound and the lower bounds at exposed
slopes are separated in \Cref{thm:full-fiber-ldp} from the new finite-window
orbit-filling argument, which supplies the missing lower bounds at the
nonexposed coexistence slopes.
The marked critical renewal identity is a bivariate restatement of
\Cref{prop:weighted-generator-renewal}, whereas its conditional consequences
are additional finite-window results.  Blackwell's renewal theorem is used as
a classical input and is not reproved.

In the moment theory, \Cref{thm:difference-power-sums} is a reindexing of the
pointwise formula, and \Cref{thm:fibonacci-window-sandwich} is an elementary
truncation bound.  The asymptotic constants in \Cref{thm:all-q-transfer} are a
direct transfer of Sanna's theorem.  The endpoint argument in
\Cref{cor:renyi-endpoint} is an alternative proof of Sanna's endpoint law,
while \Cref{thm:diagonal-high-moments} is a diagonal consequence of the same norm
squeeze.  The independent contribution in the quadratic case is the direct
finite-window derivation of \Cref{thm:cubic-recurrence}; the governing cubic
was already present in work of Chow and Jones \cite{ChowJones2024}.

\subsection{Related work}

The uniqueness theorem underlying the legal expansion is due to Zeckendorf
\cite{Zeckendorf1972}; Lekkerkerker's work provides classical background on
the same numeration system \cite{Lekkerkerker1952}.  Berstel developed the
normalization and automata viewpoint for Fibonacci representations
\cite{Berstel2001}.  The representation-counting function itself has been
studied through recurrence formulas, partition identities, extremal bounds,
and automata by Carlitz \cite{Carlitz1968}, Robbins \cite{Robbins1996},
Bicknell-Johnson and Fielder \cite{BicknellJohnsonFielder1999}, Stockmeyer
\cite{Stockmeyer2008}, Chow and Slattery \cite{ChowSlattery2021}, and Shallit
\cite{Shallit2021}.  Weinstein's orbit and level-set analysis
\cite{Weinstein2016} and the exact extremal classification of Koc\'abov\'a,
Mas\'akov\'a, and Pelantov\'a \cite{KocabovaMasakovaPelantova2005} are the
specific inputs to \Cref{sec:extremal-ldp}.  Sanna proved the power-sum
asymptotics used here and the endpoint law
\(\lim_{q\to\infty}\lambda_q^{1/q}=\varphi^{1/2}\)
\cite[Thms.~1.1 and~1.2]{Sanna2025}; Zhou gives a broader recent treatment of
representation functions for related numeration systems \cite{Zhou2023}.
Chow and Jones obtained the same cubic that appears in the quadratic
finite-window recurrence in their analysis of the variance of the partition
function \cite{ChowJones2024}.
The Stern--Brocot pressure and its Knauf-model realization are taken from
Kesseb\"ohmer--Stratmann and Fiala--Kleban--\"Ozl\"uk
\cite{KessebohmerStratmann2007,FialaKlebanOzluk2003}.  The earlier
Stern--Brocot multifractal theorem of Kesseb\"ohmer and Stratmann
\cite{KessebohmerStratmann2004} concerns interval growth rates, rather than
Fibonacci partition multiplicities or the finite-window uniform law.  The
critical continued-fraction moment uses the fixed-denominator mean theorem of Panov and
Liehl, as recorded in the quantitative modern treatment of
Aistleitner--Borda--Hauke
\cite{Panov1982,Liehl1983,AistleitnerBordaHauke2023}.

\subsection{Organization}

\Cref{sec:preliminaries} defines the finite-window fold and fixes notation.
\Cref{sec:residue-affine} proves the residue, affine, and partition-difference
formulas.  \Cref{sec:extremal-ldp} proves the interval identity, transfers the
extremal classification, establishes the freezing theorem and compact-operator
obstruction, and isolates the large-deviation interface at the pressure
corner.
\Cref{sec:local-spectrum} proves local spectral stabilization, the exact
unnormalized partition function and Gibbs law, the orbit-count Tauberian
asymptotic, the all-real pressure theorem, and the full large deviation
principle across the pressure corner.
\Cref{sec:second-collision} proves the moment transfer and the quadratic
recurrence.  \Cref{sec:integer-pressure} records Sanna's endpoint law with an
alternative proof and isolates the diagonal high-tilt consequence.

\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}

The finite-window fiber spectrum admits two exact descriptions.  The affine
permutation \(\pi_m\) identifies each multiplicity with a coefficient of a
finite Fibonacci subset-sum polynomial and with a Fibonacci-lag difference of
the indexed partition function.  The interval identity identifies the same
fiber list with ordinary partition values on two consecutive Fibonacci
layers.  The latter correspondence transfers the published extremal
classification, after an explicit convention change, and yields
\[
 D_{2k}=F_{k+2},\qquad D_{2k+1}=2F_{k+1},\qquad
 D_m=D_{m-2}+D_{m-4}\quad(m\ge6),
\]
together with the complete maximizing-residue list.

For positive integer orders, the adjacent-window sandwich transfers Sanna's
partition power-sum constants to the finite-window moments.  Sanna's endpoint
law \(\lambda_q^{1/q}\to\varphi^{1/2}\) also follows here from the maximum-norm
squeeze; the separate consequence of that squeeze is the diagonal limit for
\(q_m\to\infty\), and indeed for arbitrary real \(t_m\to\infty\).  The
quadratic moment has a direct cubic recurrence that does not use finite-state
normalization.

For negative real tilts, Weinstein's orbit counts give the frozen half-line
\[
 P(t)=0\qquad(t\le-\sigma_0),
 \qquad
 \frac{\zeta(\sigma_0-1)}{\zeta(\sigma_0)}=2.
\]
The uniform layer-count lemma treats the two unit conventions, the levels
\(1\) and \(2\), shared endpoints, and early layers explicitly.  Analytic
perturbation theory then rules out a representation of all real pressure
limits by the positive simple isolated eigenvalue of an analytic compact
operator family satisfying the stated local gap condition.
At the unnormalized scale, however, the local spectrum stabilizes exactly and
\[
 S_{-s}(m)\longrightarrow
 \frac4{2-\zeta(s-1)/\zeta(s)}-2
 \qquad(s>\sigma_0).
\]
This function has a simple pole at \(\sigma_0\), produces an explicit
total-variation Gibbs limit, and yields the Tauberian law
\(\sum_{k\le x}\Psi(k)\sim
x^{\sigma_0}/(\sigma_0\kappa)\).  A separate Jensen lower bound proves
positive lower exponential growth for \(t>-\beta_*\), where
\(\beta_*=2.2705594539\ldots\).  The weighted free-generator renewal equation,
together with the published Stern--Brocot denominator pressure, proves
existence for every real tilt and gives
\[
 \{t:P(t)>0\}=(-\sigma_0,\infty).
\]
On this positive phase, \(P\) is real analytic and strictly convex.  At the
freezing point its left derivative is zero and its right derivative is a
finite positive continued-fraction moment ratio, so the transition is not
\(C^1\).

The derivative jump prevents the G\"artner--Ellis theorem alone from treating
the intervening slopes.  A finite-window orbit-filling construction supplies
their matching local lower bounds and proves a full large deviation principle;
its good rate function is affine on the coexistence interval.  The marked
critical renewal measure further resolves the conditional geometry inside
this interval.  In a fixed band centered at \(\alpha\), the log multiplicity
selects the upper edge \(\alpha+\varepsilon\), and the generating-layer ratio
converges to \((\alpha+\varepsilon)/v_c\).  This yields the iterated limit
\(\alpha/v_c\), remains valid for shrinking windows with
\(\liminf m\varepsilon_m>0\), and cannot in general have exponential
concentration because the letters \(1/q\) produce a polynomial upper tail.
Along the exact dyadic multiplier ray, a separate arithmetic renewal law gives
the generating-word count \(3^{r-1}\), the dyadic cost velocity \(\gamma_2\),
and its finite-window transfer whenever the layer budget has positive linear
margin.  The explicit certificate
\(v_2-v_c\ge0.001171960512764161\) strictly separates the dyadic quenched
velocity from the critical annealed velocity.  Hence exact dyadic levels give
realizable sequences for which the generating-layer proportion is
\(\alpha/v_2\), rather than the critical-band value \(\alpha/v_c\).
No
 transducer-based collision kernel, algebraicity statement for \(\lambda_q\),
arithmetic-window
recurrence polynomial, exact-kernel certificate, Galois group, or Chebotarev
density is claimed in this paper.

\section{Finite-window fibers}\label{sec:preliminaries}

Throughout, the Fibonacci numbers are indexed by
\[
  F_0=0,
  \qquad
  F_1=F_2=1,
  \qquad
  F_{n+2}=F_{n+1}+F_n
  \qquad (n\ge 0).
\]

\subsection{Stable words and the residue model}

\begin{definition}[Stable words and visible values]
\label{def:stable-words}
For \(m\ge 0\), let
\[
  \Omega_m:=\{0,1\}^m,
  \qquad
  X_m:=\{x\in\Omega_m:\ x_ix_{i+1}=0\text{ for }1\le i<m\}.
\]
For \(x\in X_m\), define
\[
  V_m(x):=\sum_{j=1}^m x_jF_{j+1}.
\]
For \(\omega=\omega_1\cdots \omega_m\in\Omega_m\), define
\[
  N_m(\omega):=\sum_{j=1}^m \omega_jF_{j+1}.
\]
Thus \(\Omega_0=X_0=\{\varepsilon\}\), where \(\varepsilon\) is the empty word,
and \(V_0(\varepsilon)=N_0(\varepsilon)=0\).
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Visible values are complete]
\label{prop:Vm-bijection}
For every \(m\ge 0\), the map
\[
  V_m\colon X_m\longrightarrow \{0,1,\dots,F_{m+2}-1\}
\]
is a bijection.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The zero word maps to \(0\), so it remains to treat
\(n\in\{1,\dots,F_{m+2}-1\}\).  By Zeckendorf's theorem
\cite{Zeckendorf1972}, \(n\) has a unique
expansion as a sum of nonconsecutive Fibonacci numbers.  Because
\(n<F_{m+2}\), that expansion cannot use the summand \(F_{m+2}\) or any larger
Fibonacci number; therefore it uses only the weights \(F_2,\dots,F_{m+1}\),
and hence determines a unique word \(x\in X_m\) with \(V_m(x)=n\).
Conversely every \(x\in X_m\) is a stable expansion in those same weights, so
\(V_m(x)\in\{0,\dots,F_{m+2}-1\}\).  Thus \(V_m\) is bijective.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Finite-window fold]
\label{def:fold-map}
For \(m\ge 0\), define
\[
  \Fold_m(\omega):=
  V_m^{-1}\bigl(N_m(\omega)\bmod F_{m+2}\bigr),
  \qquad \omega\in\Omega_m.
\]
For \(x\in X_m\), define the fiber multiplicity
\[
  d_m(x):=\card{\Fold_m^{-1}(x)}.
\]
In particular, \(\Fold_0(\varepsilon)=\varepsilon\) and
\(d_0(\varepsilon)=1\).
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Single overflow]
\label{prop:single-overflow}
For every \(m\ge0\) and every \(\omega\in\Omega_m\),
\[
  N_m(\omega)\le \sum_{j=1}^m F_{j+1}=F_{m+3}-2<2F_{m+2}.
\]
Hence there exist unique integers \(b\in\{0,1\}\) and \(r\in[0,F_{m+2})\) such
that
\[
  N_m(\omega)=bF_{m+2}+r,
\]
and
\[
  \Fold_m(\omega)=V_m^{-1}(r).
\]
Equivalently, there is a unique overflow bit \(b_m(\omega)\in\{0,1\}\) such
that
\[
  N_m(\omega)=V_m(\Fold_m(\omega))+b_m(\omega)F_{m+2}.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The Fibonacci identity
\(\sum_{j=1}^m F_{j+1}=F_{m+3}-2\) is standard, and
\(F_{m+3}-2<F_{m+3}-1<F_{m+3}=F_{m+2}+F_{m+1}<2F_{m+2}\).  The displayed
formula for \(\Fold_m\) is simply the definition.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Operational meaning of the fold]
\label{rem:prefix-normalization}
Because only one overflow can occur, the finite-window fold agrees with
computing the full Zeckendorf expansion of \(N_m(\omega)\) and deleting the
possible leading \(F_{m+2}\)-digit.
The residue definition is the useful one for exact fiber analysis.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Residue characterization of fibers]
\label{cor:residue-characterization}
For \(x\in X_m\), write \(r_x:=V_m(x)\).  Then
\[
  \Fold_m(\omega)=x
  \iff
  N_m(\omega)\equiv r_x \pmod{F_{m+2}}.
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
By definition,
\(\Fold_m(\omega)=V_m^{-1}(N_m(\omega)\bmod F_{m+2})\), and \(V_m\) is
bijective by \Cref{prop:Vm-bijection}.
\end{proof}

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\caption{Notation used throughout the paper.  The letter \(q\) is reserved
for a nonnegative integer moment order, whereas \(t\) denotes a real tilt.}
\label{tab:notation}
\begin{tabular}{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule
Symbol & Meaning \\
\midrule
\(\Omega_m\), \(X_m\) & raw binary words and legal visible words \\
\(\Fold_m\) & residue-defined finite-window fold \\
\(d_m(x)\), \(d_m^\#(r)\) & fiber multiplicity, indexed by a word or residue \\
\(\widetilde d_m(n)\) & coefficient of \(\prod_{j=1}^m(1+z^{F_j})\) \\
\(R(n)\), \(R^\dagger(n)\) & ordinary and indexed Fibonacci partition counts \\
\(S_q(m)\), \(S_t(m)\) & integer-order moment and real-tilt sum \\
\(T_q^\dagger(N)\) & indexed partition power sum below \(N\) \\
\(D_m\) & largest fiber multiplicity \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\section{Affine transfer and partition differences}\label{sec:residue-affine}

\subsection{Fourier formulas for the fibers}

\begin{theorem}[Residue fibers]
\label{thm:residue-fibers}
For every \(x\in X_m\), with \(r_x:=V_m(x)\),
\[
  d_m(x)
  =
  \card{\Bigl\{\omega\in\Omega_m:
  \sum_{j=1}^m\omega_jF_{j+1}\equiv r_x \pmod{F_{m+2}}\Bigr\}}.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
This is exactly \Cref{cor:residue-characterization}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Fourier inversion]
\label{thm:fourier-inversion}
Let \(\zeta=e^{2\pi i/F_{m+2}}\).  Then for every \(x\in X_m\),
\[
  d_m(x)=\frac{1}{F_{m+2}}
  \sum_{a=0}^{F_{m+2}-1}
  \zeta^{-ar_x}\prod_{j=1}^m(1+\zeta^{aF_{j+1}}).
\]
Consequently, if
\[
  \Sigma_m:=\sum_{x\in X_m}d_m(x)^2,
\]
then
\[
  \Sigma_m=
  \frac{1}{F_{m+2}}
  \sum_{a=0}^{F_{m+2}-1}
  \left|
    \prod_{j=1}^m(1+\zeta^{aF_{j+1}})
  \right|^2.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
In the finite cyclic group \(\ZZ/F_{m+2}\ZZ\),
\[
  \mathbf{1}_{n\equiv r_x}
  =
  \frac{1}{F_{m+2}}
  \sum_{a=0}^{F_{m+2}-1}\zeta^{a(n-r_x)}.
\]
Therefore
\[
  d_m(x)
  =
  \sum_{\omega\in\Omega_m}\mathbf{1}_{N_m(\omega)\equiv r_x}
  =
  \frac{1}{F_{m+2}}
  \sum_{a=0}^{F_{m+2}-1}
  \zeta^{-ar_x}
  \sum_{\omega\in\Omega_m}\zeta^{aN_m(\omega)}.
\]
Since \(N_m(\omega)=\sum_{j=1}^m \omega_jF_{j+1}\),
\[
  \sum_{\omega\in\Omega_m}\zeta^{aN_m(\omega)}
  =
  \prod_{j=1}^m\sum_{\omega_j\in\{0,1\}}\zeta^{a\omega_jF_{j+1}}
  =
  \prod_{j=1}^m(1+\zeta^{aF_{j+1}}),
\]
which proves the first formula.

For the second formula, use that \(d_m(x)\in\RR\), so
\[
  \Sigma_m=\sum_{x\in X_m}|d_m(x)|^2 .
\]
Multiply the first identity by its complex conjugate and sum over \(x\in X_m\):
\[
  \Sigma_m
  =
  \frac{1}{F_{m+2}^2}
  \sum_{a,b=0}^{F_{m+2}-1}
  \left(
    \prod_{j=1}^m(1+\zeta^{aF_{j+1}})
  \right)
  \overline{
    \left(
      \prod_{j=1}^m(1+\zeta^{bF_{j+1}})
    \right)
  }
  \sum_{x\in X_m}\zeta^{(b-a)r_x}.
\]
Because \(V_m\) is a bijection onto \(\{0,\dots,F_{m+2}-1\}\), the residues
\(\{r_x:x\in X_m\}\) are exactly the full residue system.  Hence the character
orthogonality relation
\[
  \sum_{x\in X_m}\zeta^{(b-a)r_x}
  =
  \sum_{r=0}^{F_{m+2}-1}\zeta^{(b-a)r}
  =
  \begin{cases}
    F_{m+2}, & a=b,\\
    0, & a\neq b
  \end{cases}
\]
eliminates the cross terms and gives
\[
  \Sigma_m
  =
  \frac{1}{F_{m+2}}
  \sum_{a=0}^{F_{m+2}-1}
  \left|
    \prod_{j=1}^m(1+\zeta^{aF_{j+1}})
  \right|^2,
\]
as claimed.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Affine transfer to Fibonacci subset sums}

\begin{definition}[Shifted subset-sum spectrum]
\label{def:shifted-spectrum}
Define
\[
  \widetilde N_m(\omega):=\sum_{j=1}^m \omega_jF_j
  \qquad (\omega\in\Omega_m),
\]
and
\[
  \widetilde d_m(r):=
  \card{\{\omega\in\Omega_m:\ \widetilde N_m(\omega)=r\}},
  \qquad 0\le r<F_{m+2}.
\]
Because \(\sum_{j=1}^m F_j=F_{m+2}-1\), no reduction modulo \(F_{m+2}\) is
needed in this definition.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[The affine residue permutation]
\label{def:affine-map}
Define \(\tau_m\colon \Omega_m\to\Omega_m\) by
\[
  (\tau_m(\omega))_k=
  \begin{cases}
    \omega_{m+1-k}, & k\equiv m \pmod 2,\\[4pt]
    1-\omega_{m+1-k}, & k\not\equiv m \pmod 2,
  \end{cases}
  \qquad 1\le k\le m.
\]
Define
\[
  C_m:=\sum_{\substack{1\le k\le m\\ k\not\equiv m \,(\mathrm{mod}\,2)}}F_{k+1},
  \qquad
  \pi_m(r)\equiv F_{m+1}r+C_m \pmod{F_{m+2}}.
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Closed form for the translation term]
\label{lem:Cm-closed}
For every \(m\ge 1\),
\[
  C_m=F_{m+1}-1.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If \(m=2q\), then
\[
  C_m=F_2+F_4+\cdots+F_{2q}=F_{2q+1}-1=F_{m+1}-1.
\]
If \(m=2q+1\), then
\[
  C_m=F_3+F_5+\cdots+F_{2q+1}=F_{2q+2}-1=F_{m+1}-1.
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Affine transfer principle]
\label{thm:affine-transfer}
For every \(m\ge 1\), the map \(\pi_m\) is a permutation of
\(\ZZ/F_{m+2}\ZZ\), and
\[
  \widetilde d_m(r)=d_m^\#(\pi_m(r)),
  \qquad
  d_m^\#(s):=d_m(V_m^{-1}(s)).
\]
Equivalently, the multiset of fiber multiplicities of \(\Fold_m\) agrees with
the coefficient multiset of
\[
  \prod_{j=1}^m(1+z^{F_j})
\]
up to the explicit affine permutation \(\pi_m\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
We use d'Ocagne's identity
\[
  F_uF_{v+1}-F_{u+1}F_v=(-1)^vF_{u-v}.
\]
Taking \(u=m+1\) and \(v=j-1\) gives
\[
  F_{m+1}F_j-F_{m+2}F_{j-1}=(-1)^{j-1}F_{m+2-j},
\]
hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:docagne-window}
  F_{m+1}F_j\equiv (-1)^{j-1}F_{m+2-j}\pmod{F_{m+2}}.
\end{equation}

Now
\[
  N_m(\tau_m(\omega))
  =
  \sum_{\substack{1\le k\le m\\ k\equiv m \,(\mathrm{mod}\,2)}}
  \omega_{m+1-k}F_{k+1}
  +
  \sum_{\substack{1\le k\le m\\ k\not\equiv m \,(\mathrm{mod}\,2)}}
  (1-\omega_{m+1-k})F_{k+1}.
\]
Separate the constant term:
\[
  N_m(\tau_m(\omega))
  =
  \sum_{k=1}^m(-1)^{m-k}\omega_{m+1-k}F_{k+1}+C_m.
\]
After the change of variables \(j=m+1-k\),
\[
  N_m(\tau_m(\omega))
  =
  \sum_{j=1}^m(-1)^{j-1}\omega_jF_{m+2-j}+C_m.
\]
Applying \eqref{eq:docagne-window} term by term yields
\[
  N_m(\tau_m(\omega))
  \equiv
  F_{m+1}\sum_{j=1}^m \omega_jF_j + C_m
  =
  F_{m+1}\widetilde N_m(\omega)+C_m
  \pmod{F_{m+2}}.
\]
Thus
\begin{equation}\label{eq:tau-congruence}
  N_m(\tau_m(\omega))\equiv \pi_m(\widetilde N_m(\omega))\pmod{F_{m+2}}.
\end{equation}

Since \(\gcd(F_{m+1},F_{m+2})=1\), multiplication by \(F_{m+1}\) is invertible
modulo \(F_{m+2}\), so \(\pi_m\) is a permutation.  Because \(\tau_m\) is also
a bijection of \(\Omega_m\), for every residue \(r\),
\begin{align*}
  \widetilde d_m(r)
  &=
  \card{\{\omega\in\Omega_m:\ \widetilde N_m(\omega)=r\}} \\
  &=
  \card{
    \left\{
      \omega\in\Omega_m:
      N_m(\tau_m(\omega))\equiv \pi_m(r)\pmod{F_{m+2}}
    \right\}
  }.
\end{align*}
By \Cref{thm:residue-fibers}, the last quantity is precisely
\(d_m^\#(\pi_m(r))\).
\end{proof}

\begin{intervalcompletenesslemma}
For every \(m\ge1\), every integer \(r\) with \(0\le r<F_{m+2}\) is a
subset sum of \(F_1,\ldots,F_m\).  Consequently,
\[
  \widetilde d_m(r)\ge1\qquad(0\le r<F_{m+2})
\]
and every fiber of \(\Fold_m\) is nonempty.
\end{intervalcompletenesslemma}

\begin{proof}
We argue by induction on \(m\).  For \(m=1\), the subset sums of \(F_1\) are
\(0\) and \(1\), which constitute the interval
\([0,F_3-1]\cap\ZZ\).  Suppose that the assertion holds for \(m-1\), where
\(m\ge2\).  The subset sums formed from \(F_1,\ldots,F_m\) include both
integer intervals
\[
  [0,F_{m+1}-1]
  \quad\text{and}\quad
  F_m+[0,F_{m+1}-1]=[F_m,F_{m+2}-1].
\]
Since \(F_m<F_{m+1}\), these intervals overlap, and their union is
\([0,F_{m+2}-1]\).  This proves the induction step and the inequality for
\(\widetilde d_m\).  The affine transfer principle, \Cref{thm:affine-transfer},
then gives
\[
  d_m^\#(\pi_m(r))=\widetilde d_m(r)\ge1.
\]
Because \(\pi_m\) permutes all residues and \(V_m\) is bijective, every fiber
is nonempty.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Coefficient-spectrum form of the fibers]
\label{cor:coefficient-spectrum}
As multisets,
\[
  \{d_m(x):x\in X_m\}
  =
  \Bigl\{
    [z^r]\prod_{j=1}^m(1+z^{F_j}) : 0\le r<F_{m+2}
  \Bigr\}.
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The coefficient \([z^r]\prod_{j=1}^m(1+z^{F_j})\) is exactly
\(\widetilde d_m(r)\).  Apply \Cref{thm:affine-transfer}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The partition-difference formula}

\begin{definition}[Fibonacci partition functions]
\label{def:partition-functions}
Let \(R(n)\) denote the number of partitions of \(n\) into distinct Fibonacci
numbers, where the two unit weights \(F_1=F_2=1\) are not distinguished, and
set \(R(n)=0\) for \(n<0\).  Following the indexed model in which the two unit
weights are distinguished, define
\[
  R^\dagger(n):=R(n)+R(n-1)
  \qquad (n\in\ZZ).
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Partition-difference formula]
\label{thm:partition-difference}
For every \(m\ge1\) and every integer \(n\) with
\(0\le n<F_{m+2}\),
\[
  d_m^\#(\pi_m(n))
  =
  R^\dagger(n)-R^\dagger(n-F_{m+1}).
\]
Equivalently,
\[
  d_m^\#(\pi_m(n))
  =
  R(n)+R(n-1)-R(n-F_{m+1})-R(n-F_{m+1}-1).
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By \Cref{thm:affine-transfer},
\[
  d_m^\#(\pi_m(n))=\widetilde d_m(n),
\]
where \(\widetilde d_m(n)\) is the coefficient of \(z^n\) in
\[
  \mathcal P_m(z):=\prod_{j=1}^m(1+z^{F_j}).
\]
Thus
\begin{equation}\label{eq:shifted-coefficient}
  d_m^\#(\pi_m(n))=[z^n]\mathcal P_m(z).
\end{equation}

The ordinary Fibonacci partition function has generating series
\[
  \sum_{n\ge 0}R(n)z^n=(1+z)\prod_{j\ge 3}(1+z^{F_j}),
\]
because the unit weight occurs only once.  Hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:indexed-generating}
  \sum_{n\ge 0}R^\dagger(n)z^n
  =
  (1+z)\sum_{n\ge 0}R(n)z^n
  =
  \prod_{j\ge 1}(1+z^{F_j}).
\end{equation}
If \(0\le n<F_{m+2}\), then the coefficient of \(z^n\) in the infinite product
\(\prod_{j\ge 1}(1+z^{F_j})\) depends only on the factors with \(j\le m+1\),
since \(F_{m+2}\) and all later Fibonacci numbers exceed \(n\).  Therefore
\[
  R^\dagger(n)
  =
  [z^n](1+z^{F_{m+1}})\mathcal P_m(z)
  =
  [z^n]\mathcal P_m(z)+[z^{n-F_{m+1}}]\mathcal P_m(z).
\]
The second coefficient is \(R^\dagger(n-F_{m+1})\), since
\(n-F_{m+1}<F_{m+1}<F_{m+2}\).  Using \eqref{eq:shifted-coefficient}, we
obtain
\[
  d_m^\#(\pi_m(n))
  =
  R^\dagger(n)-R^\dagger(n-F_{m+1}),
\]
where the second term is interpreted as \(0\) for \(n<F_{m+1}\).  Expanding
\(R^\dagger(t)=R(t)+R(t-1)\) yields the four-term formula.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[A four-digit window]
\label{ex:m4-partition-difference}
Take \(m=4\).  Then \(F_5=5\), \(F_6=8\), and
\[
  \pi_4(n)\equiv 5n+4 \pmod 8.
\]
Moreover,
\[
  \prod_{j=1}^4(1+z^{F_j})
  =
  (1+z)^2(1+z^2)(1+z^3)
  =
  \begin{aligned}[t]
  &1+2z+2z^2+3z^3\\
  &\quad{}+3z^4+2z^5+2z^6+z^7,
  \end{aligned}
\]
so the difference spectrum is
\[
  \bigl(R^\dagger(n)-R^\dagger(n-5)\bigr)_{n=0}^{7}
  =
  (1,2,2,3,3,2,2,1).
\]
Applying \(\pi_4\) sends \(0,1,\dots,7\) to \(4,1,6,3,0,5,2,7\), and hence
\[
  \bigl(d_4^\#(r)\bigr)_{r=0}^{7}
  =
  (3,2,2,3,1,2,2,1).
\]
Thus the affine permutation simply reindexes the coefficient list of
\(\prod_{j=1}^4(1+z^{F_j})\) into the finite-window fiber spectrum.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}[Single-fiber formulas from classical partition theory]
\label{rem:single-fiber-literature}
Theorem~\ref{thm:partition-difference} identifies each fiber multiplicity with
a Fibonacci-lag difference of the classical partition function.  Hence the
exact Zeckendorf formulas of Chow and Slattery \cite{ChowSlattery2021} apply
directly to \(d_m^\#(\pi_m(n))\).
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Complement symmetry]
\label{prop:complement-symmetry}
Let
\[
  B_m:=\sum_{j=1}^mF_{j+1}=F_{m+3}-2.
\]
Then, as a function on \(\ZZ/F_{m+2}\ZZ\),
\[
  d_m^\#(r)=d_m^\#(B_m-r).
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let \(\iota(\omega)_j:=1-\omega_j\).  Then \(\iota\) is a bijection of
\(\Omega_m\), and
\[
  N_m(\iota(\omega))
  =
  \sum_{j=1}^m (1-\omega_j)F_{j+1}
  =
  B_m-N_m(\omega).
\]
Hence
\[
  N_m(\omega)\equiv r \pmod{F_{m+2}}
  \iff
  N_m(\iota(\omega))\equiv B_m-r \pmod{F_{m+2}}.
\]
Apply \Cref{thm:residue-fibers}.
\end{proof}

\section{Unconditional extremal fibers and the real-tilt interface}
\label{sec:extremal-ldp}

The affine coefficient model gives one description of the fiber spectrum.  For
extremal questions there is a second, particularly rigid description: the
fibers are consecutive values of the ordinary Fibonacci partition function on
two explicit intervals.  This removes the recurrence assumptions that are
sometimes imposed in experimental treatments of the largest fibers.

\subsection{An exact interval model}

\begin{theorem}[Fiber--partition interval identity]
\label{thm:fiber-partition-interval}
Let \(m\ge0\) and \(0\le r<F_{m+2}\) be integers.  Then
\[
 d_m^\#(r)=
 \begin{cases}
 R(F_{m+2}+r),&0\le r\le F_{m+1}-2,\\
 R(r),&F_{m+1}-1\le r<F_{m+2}.
 \end{cases}
\]
An interval with lower endpoint larger than its upper endpoint is understood
to be empty.  In particular, this convention covers \(m=0,1\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The raw value \(N_m(\omega)\) is a sum of a subset of
\(F_2,F_3,\ldots,F_{m+1}\).  Since the single unit weight is \(F_2\), the
number of words with raw value \(n<F_{m+2}\) is exactly \(R(n)\).  By
\Cref{prop:single-overflow}, a word in the fiber over residue \(r\) has raw
value either \(r\) or \(F_{m+2}+r\).

Suppose first that \(0\le r\le F_{m+1}-2\).  Then
\[
 F_{m+2}+r\le F_{m+3}-2<F_{m+3}.
\]
Consequently every ordinary Fibonacci partition of \(F_{m+2}+r\) uses only
parts from \(F_2,\ldots,F_{m+2}\).  Those using \(F_{m+2}\) are in bijection,
by deletion of that part, with the \(R(r)\) partitions of \(r\).  Those not
using \(F_{m+2}\) are precisely the raw words of value \(F_{m+2}+r\).  The
fiber also contains the \(R(r)\) raw words of value \(r\).  Therefore the two
copies of \(R(r)\) cancel between this decomposition and the fiber sum, giving
\[
 d_m^\#(r)
 =R(r)+\bigl(R(F_{m+2}+r)-R(r)\bigr)
 =R(F_{m+2}+r).
\]

Now suppose that \(F_{m+1}-1\le r<F_{m+2}\).  The largest possible raw value is
\[
 \sum_{j=1}^m F_{j+1}=F_{m+3}-2,
\]
whereas
\[
 F_{m+2}+r\ge F_{m+3}-1.
\]
Thus no word in this fiber can have the overflow value \(F_{m+2}+r\).  Every
partition of \(r<F_{m+2}\) uses only \(F_2,\ldots,F_{m+1}\), so the remaining
raw words are counted by \(R(r)\).  This proves the second case.
\end{proof}

For a compact statement of the maximizing residues, put
\[
 G_j:=F_{j+1}\qquad(j\ge0),
\]
so \(G_0=G_1=1\) and \(G_{j+1}=G_j+G_{j-1}\).
For \(k\ge5\), define
\[
 I_{2k+1}:=
 \begin{cases}
 G_{k+1}G_{k-3}+1,&k\text{ even},\\
 G_kG_{k-2}+1,&k\text{ odd}.
 \end{cases}
\]
For \(k\ge7\), define
\[
 I_{2k}:=
\begin{cases}
 G_{k+2}G_{k-5}+G_3+1,&k\text{ even},\\
 G_{k+1}G_{k-4}+G_3+1,&k\text{ odd},
 \end{cases}
\]
\[
 J_{2k}:=
 \begin{cases}
 G_{k+1}G_{k-3}+1,&k\text{ even},\\
 G_kG_{k-2}+1,&k\text{ odd}.
 \end{cases}
\]

\begin{conventionlemma}
Let \((G_j)_{j\ge0}\) denote the convention of
\cite{KocabovaMasakovaPelantova2005}, namely \(G_0=G_1=1\), with
representations using the parts \(G_1,G_2,\ldots\).  Then
\(G_j=F_{j+1}\) for every \(j\ge0\), and the source representation-count
function is exactly \(R\).  In particular, the source formulas
\[
 \max_{G_{2a+1}\le n<G_{2a+2}}R(n)=G_{a+1},\qquad
 \max_{G_{2a+2}\le n<G_{2a+3}}R(n)=2G_a
\]
become
\[
 \max_{F_{2a+2}\le n<F_{2a+3}}R(n)=F_{a+2},\qquad
 \max_{F_{2a+3}\le n<F_{2a+4}}R(n)=2F_{a+1}.
\]
The generic maximizing arguments in the source are
\[
 G_{2k+1}-1+I_{2k+1},\qquad G_{2k+2}-1-I_{2k+1}
\]
in odd source length \(2k+1\), and
\[
 \begin{split}
 G_{2k}-1+I_{2k},&\quad G_{2k+1}-1-I_{2k},\\
 G_{2k}-1+J_{2k},&\quad G_{2k+1}-1-J_{2k}
 \end{split}
\]
in even source length \(2k\).  The exceptional source arguments and their
translated fiber residues are
\[
 \begin{array}{c|c|c}
 \text{source length}&\text{partition arguments}&\text{fiber residues}\\ \hline
 9&63,71,79&8,16,24\\
 12&270,296,304,312,338&37,63,71,79,105.
 \end{array}
\]
Here the last column subtracts \(G_9=F_{10}=55\) or
\(G_{12}=F_{13}=233\), respectively.
\end{conventionlemma}

\begin{proof}
The identity \(G_j=F_{j+1}\) follows from the initial values and recurrence.
The admissible source parts \(G_1,G_2,\ldots\) are therefore exactly our
parts \(F_2,F_3,\ldots\), including a single unit part.  The maximum and
argument formulas are Theorems~4.7 and~5.3 of
\cite{KocabovaMasakovaPelantova2005}; direct substitution gives the displayed
formulas and endpoint subtractions.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exact largest fibers]
\label{thm:unconditional-extremal-fibers}
Let \(D_m=\max_{x\in X_m}d_m(x)\).  Then
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 D_0=D_1=1,\qquad D_{2k}=F_{k+2}\quad(k\ge0),\\
 D_{2k+1}=2F_{k+1}\quad(k\ge1).
 \end{gathered}
\]
No auxiliary recurrence hypothesis is assumed.

For every \(m\ge0\), a visible word \(x\in X_m\) is a maximizer if and only if
its residue \(r=V_m(x)\) belongs to the following set.  If \(m=2k\ge10\), the
set is
\[
 \mathcal M_{2k}
 =\{I_{2k+1}-1,\;G_{2k}-1-I_{2k+1}\}.
\]
If \(m=2k-1\ge13\), the set is
\[
 \mathcal M_{2k-1}
 =\{I_{2k}-1,\;J_{2k}-1,\;
       G_{2k-1}-1-I_{2k},\;G_{2k-1}-1-J_{2k}\}.
\]
The remaining maximizing sets are
\[
\begin{array}{c|c|l}
m&D_m&\mathcal M_m\\ \hline
0&1&\{0\}\\
1&1&\{0,1\}\\
2&2&\{0\}\\
3&2&\{0,1,3\}\\
4&3&\{0,3\}\\
5&4&\{3\}\\
6&5&\{3,8\}\\
7&6&\{3,8,11,16\}\\
8&8&\{8,16,24\}\\
9&10&\{16,24,29,37\}\\
11&16&\{37,63,71,79,105\}.
\end{array}
\]
The windows \(m=10\) and \(m=12\) are covered by the generic even formula
for \(\mathcal M_{2k}\); they require no additional exceptional rows.
In particular, apart from the displayed initial exceptions, the degeneracy of
the maximum is exactly \(2\) in even windows and exactly \(4\) in odd windows.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let
\[
 M_\ell:=\max_{F_{\ell+1}\le n<F_{\ell+2}}R(n).
\]
By the Fibonacci-convention lemma, Theorem~4.7 of
\cite{KocabovaMasakovaPelantova2005} gives
\[
 M_{2a+1}=F_{a+2}\quad(a\ge0),
 \qquad
 M_{2a+2}=2F_{a+1}\quad(a\ge1),
\]
with \(M_1=M_2=1\).
The same paper classifies every argument attaining these maxima
\cite[Thm.~5.3]{KocabovaMasakovaPelantova2005}.

By \Cref{thm:fiber-partition-interval}, the first block of the fiber list is
\[
 \bigl(R(n)\bigr)_{F_{m+2}\le n\le F_{m+3}-2}.
\]
Its maximum is \(M_{m+1}\).  The second block consists of values \(R(n)\) with
\(F_{m+1}-1\le n<F_{m+2}\).  For \(m\ge4\), its maximum is at most \(M_m\), and
the displayed formulas give \(M_m<M_{m+1}\): if \(m=2a\), then
\(2F_a<F_{a+2}\), while if \(m=2a+1\), then
\(F_{a+2}<2F_{a+1}\).  Hence \(D_m=M_{m+1}\) for \(m\ge4\).  The same equality
for \(m=0,1,2,3\), with the stated exceptional degeneracies, follows by direct
enumeration of the at most eight raw words.  Substitution of \(m=2k\) and
\(m=2k+1\) into the formulas for \(M_{m+1}\) proves the asserted closed forms.

It remains to translate the arguments of the maximum.  The
Fibonacci-convention lemma states that the two generic maximizing integers of
odd source length \(2k+1\) are
\[
 G_{2k+1}-1+I_{2k+1},
 \qquad
 G_{2k+2}-1-I_{2k+1}.
\]
Subtracting the left endpoint \(G_{2k+1}=F_{m+2}\), as prescribed by the first
case of \Cref{thm:fiber-partition-interval}, gives
\(I_{2k+1}-1\) and \(G_{2k}-1-I_{2k+1}\).  Similarly, the four generic
maximizing integers of even length \(2k\) are obtained by using \(I_{2k}\) and
\(J_{2k}\) in the same two symmetric positions.  Subtraction of
\(G_{2k}=F_{m+2}\) gives the four residues displayed in the theorem.  The
exceptional-argument table in that lemma gives \(m=8\) and \(m=11\) after the
same endpoint subtraction.  Direct evaluation of the finitely many shorter
cases gives the remaining rows of the theorem.  Since \(V_m\) is bijective,
this residue list classifies the visible maximizing words themselves.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Exact two-step extremal recurrence]
\label{cor:extremal-fiber-recurrence}
For every integer \(m\ge6\),
\[
 D_m=D_{m-2}+D_{m-4}.
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
If \(m=2k\), then the three terms are \(F_{k+2},F_{k+1},F_k\), respectively.
If \(m=2k+1\), they are \(2F_{k+1},2F_k,2F_{k-1}\), respectively.  In both
cases the assertion is the Fibonacci recurrence.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Higher extremal ranks]
\label{rem:higher-extremal-ranks-open}
The interval identity is an unconditional reduction of every distinct
multiplicity level and its degeneracy to the corresponding level sets of
\(R(n)\) on the two displayed intervals.  The cited extremal theorem classifies
the first distinct level, but it does not classify the second or arbitrary
fixed \(k\)-th distinct level.  Consequently no closed formula for all top-
\(k\) levels is asserted here.  Establishing such formulas is a separate open
interface; the accompanying finite computations are not used as a substitute
for that proof.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Negative-temperature freezing and the pressure corner}

For real \(t\), define the real collision sum
\[
 S_t(m):=\sum_{x\in X_m}d_m(x)^t.
\]
This is well defined for all \(t\in\RR\), since every fiber is nonempty.  The
interval-completeness lemma following \Cref{thm:affine-transfer} proves this
nonemptiness before negative powers are introduced.  The positive integer
specializations agree with the moments in
\Cref{def:difference-spectrum}.

The following lemma records exactly the convention transfer and uniform bound
needed below.

\begin{layercountlemma}
For \(j\ge0\), set
\[
 \mathcal I_j=[F_{j+1}-1,F_{j+2}-1),\qquad
 N_j(k)=\card{\{n\in\mathcal I_j:R(n)=k\}}.
\]
Let \(\Psi(k)\) be Weinstein's number of generating orbits at level \(k\).
Then
\[
 N_0(1)=0,\qquad N_j(1)=1\quad(j\ge1),
\]
and, for every \(j\ge0\) and \(k\ge2\),
\[
 N_j(k)\le 2\Psi(k).
\]
For clarity, eventual equality is a separate statement: \(N_j(2)=2\) for
\(j\ge4\), while for \(k>2\) one has
\(N_j(k)=2\Psi(k)\) for \(j\ge2k\).  Weinstein's published theorem states
this for \(j>2k\); the endpoint \(j=2k\) follows from the largest-index
argument below.
\end{layercountlemma}

\begin{proof}
Weinstein uses \(f_0=f_1=1\)
\cite[Secs.~3 and~5]{Weinstein2016}.  Thus
\[
 f_j=F_{j+1}\qquad(j\ge0),
\]
and his half-open layer \([f_j-1,f_{j+1}-1)\) is exactly
\(\mathcal I_j\).  His partition function also equals \(R\), because its
admissible parts \(f_1,f_2,\ldots\) are our \(F_2,F_3,\ldots\).

For \(n\ge1\), write Weinstein's Zeckendorf index partition as
\[
 Z(n)=\langle z(n,1),\ldots,z(n,\lambda(n))\rangle
\]
and define its largest index by
\[
 L(n):=z(n,\lambda(n))=\max Z(n).
\]
Weinstein's Lemma~2.19, together with
\(R(f_i-1)=1\), gives the endpoint-sensitive equivalence
\begin{equation}
 R(n)>1\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \bigl(n\in[f_j-1,f_{j+1}-1)\iff L(n)=j\bigr).
 \label{eq:layer-largest-index}
\end{equation}
Indeed, after the left endpoint \(f_j-1\) and the right endpoint
\(f_{j+1}-1\), both of which have partition value \(1\), are removed,
Lemma~2.19 identifies the remaining integers in the layer with those whose
largest Zeckendorf index is \(j\).

At level \(1\), Weinstein's description
\(\{n:R(n)=1\}=\{f_i-1:i\ge1\}\), together with the half-open endpoint
convention, places exactly the left endpoint \(f_j-1\) in each
\(\mathcal I_j\) for \(j\ge1\); \(\mathcal I_0\) is empty.

At level \(2\), Weinstein gives \(\Psi(2)=1\) and the two families
\[
 f_i-1+f_{i+3},\qquad f_{i+2}-1+f_{i+4}\qquad(i\ge0).
\]
The first lies in \([f_{i+3}-1,f_{i+4}-1)\), since the distances from
the two endpoints are \(f_i\) and \(f_{i+1}\); the second lies in
\([f_{i+4}-1,f_{i+5}-1)\), with the analogous upper distance
\(f_{i+1}\).  Hence a layer contains at most one member of each family,
so \(N_j(2)\le2=2\Psi(2)\), with equality for \(j\ge4\).

Now let \(k>2\).  By Weinstein's Theorem~3.8, every solution of
\(R(n)=k\) has a unique representation
\begin{equation}
 [a]\tau^\varepsilon\sigma^\delta(g),
 \qquad
 g\in G(k),\quad a\ge0,\quad \varepsilon,\delta\in\{0,1\},
 \label{eq:weinstein-orbit-normal-form}
\end{equation}
where \(G(k)\) is the set of \(\Psi(k)\) generating solutions.  We derive the
largest-index behavior of all four branches from Weinstein's explicit
formulas~(17)--(19).

First consider a number \(n\) in Weinstein's sheet \(N_1\), and let
\(\theta(n)\) and \(Z'(n)\) denote the initial-block parameter and the
Zeckendorf tail used in those formulas.  Thus \(Z'(n)\) is a nonempty terminal
subpartition of \(Z(n)\), and in particular \(\max Z'(n)=L(n)\).  Formula~(18)
states that
\[
 [a](n)=f_{2\theta(n)-2+2a}-1+\lVert f_{Z'(n)+2a}\rVert.
\]
The definition of \(\theta(n)\) gives
\begin{equation}
 z(n,\theta(n))\ge2\theta(n).
 \label{eq:theta-tail-separation}
\end{equation}
To see this directly, if \(\theta(n)=1\), then the defining first index is at
least \(3\).  If \(\theta(n)>1\), the indices preceding
\(z(n,\theta(n))\) differ successively by \(2\), the first index is at least
\(1\), and the gap at \(\theta(n)\) is at least \(3\).  Hence
\(z(n,\theta(n))\ge1+2(\theta(n)-2)+3=2\theta(n)\).
The identity
\[
 f_{2q}-1=f_1+f_3+\cdots+f_{2q-1}\qquad(q\ge0),
\]
with the sum empty for \(q=0\), and
\eqref{eq:theta-tail-separation} show that the displayed sum is already the
Zeckendorf decomposition
\[
 Z([a]n)
 =\langle1,3,\ldots,2\theta(n)+2a-3\rangle
   \sqcup\bigl(Z'(n)+2a\bigr).
\]
The terminal subpartition contains the largest index.  Hence
\begin{equation}
 L([a]n)=L(n)+2a.
 \label{eq:L-a-shift}
\end{equation}

Formula~(19) gives
\[
 \tau(n)=
 \begin{cases}
 f_{2\theta(n)}-1+\lVert f_{Z'(n)+1}\rVert,
   &z(n,\theta(n))\ \text{odd},\\
 f_{2\theta(n)-4}-1+\lVert f_{Z'(n)-1}\rVert,
   &z(n,\theta(n))\ \text{even}.
 \end{cases}
\]
Expanding the first terms by the same identity and using
\eqref{eq:theta-tail-separation} shows that the shifted tail begins at least
two indices after the prefix and therefore supplies the largest index.
Consequently
\begin{equation}
 L(\tau n)=
 \begin{cases}
 L(n)+1,&z(n,\theta(n))\ \text{odd},\\
 L(n)-1,&z(n,\theta(n))\ \text{even},
 \end{cases}
 \label{eq:L-tau-shift}
\end{equation}
In either case,
\[
 L(\tau n)\equiv L(n)+1\pmod2.
\]
Because \(\tau n\in N_1\), applying \eqref{eq:L-a-shift} to \(\tau n\)
also gives the exact formula
\begin{equation}
 L([a]\tau n)=L(\tau n)+2a.
 \label{eq:L-a-tau-shift}
\end{equation}
Finally, the definition of Weinstein's involution \(\sigma\) adds \(1\) to
every Zeckendorf index on \(N_1\).  Therefore
\begin{equation}
 Z(\sigma n)=Z(n)+1,
 \qquad L(\sigma n)=L(n)+1
 \qquad(n\in N_1).
 \label{eq:L-sigma-shift}
\end{equation}

Fix \(g\in G(k)\) and a layer index \(j\).  By
\eqref{eq:L-a-shift} and \eqref{eq:L-a-tau-shift}, the largest indices in the
two \(N_1\)-branches are
\[
 L([a]g)=L(g)+2a,
 \qquad
 L([a]\tau g)=L(\tau g)+2a,
\]
and hence range over the two progressions
\[
 \{L(g)+2a:a\ge0\},
 \qquad
 \{L(\tau g)+2a:a\ge0\}.
\]
Each progression contains at most one occurrence of \(j\), and
\eqref{eq:L-tau-shift} gives them opposite parities.  Their union therefore
contains at most one occurrence of \(j\).  By \eqref{eq:L-sigma-shift}, the
two \(\sigma\)-branches meet layer \(j\) exactly when their corresponding
\(N_1\)-branches meet layer \(j-1\), so together they contribute at most one
additional point.  Thus the orbit of \(g\) contributes at most two points to
every layer, including the early layers.  The freeness of Weinstein's action
and the fundamental-domain property of \(G(k)\) make the generating orbits
disjoint.  Summing over them yields
\[
 N_j(k)\le2\lvert G(k)\rvert=2\Psi(k).
\]
We record the endpoint needed for eventual equality.  Weinstein's
maximal-generator lemma states that every \(g\in G(k)\) satisfies
\(g\le f_{2k}-2<f_{2k}\).  Since a Zeckendorf partition with largest index
at least \(2k\) has value at least \(f_{2k}\), it follows that
\begin{equation}
 L(g)\le2k-1.
 \label{eq:generator-largest-index-bound}
\end{equation}
Together with \eqref{eq:L-tau-shift}, this implies that the two
\(N_1\)-progressions contain exactly one point of every layer \(j\ge2k\).
They also contain one point of layer \(2k-1\): if \(L(g)\) is odd, use the
first progression, while if \(L(g)\) is even, the odd initial index
\(L(\tau g)=L(g)\mathbin{\pm}1\) is at most \(2k-1\).  Therefore, for every
\(j\ge2k\), the \(N_1\)-branches contribute one point in layer \(j\), and
their \(\sigma\)-images contribute one point because the corresponding
\(N_1\)-branches meet layer \(j-1\).  The two sheets are disjoint, so each
generating orbit contributes exactly two points.  Hence
\(N_j(k)=2\Psi(k)\) for \(j\ge2k\).  Weinstein's Theorem~5.1 supplies the
same conclusion away from the endpoint; the preceding argument proves the
additional case \(j=2k\).

Finally, Weinstein often writes the auxiliary layer with both endpoints.
The shared endpoint has partition value \(1\), so this distinction is
irrelevant for \(k\ge2\); the level \(1\) endpoint was assigned directly
above.  This completes the treatment of endpoints and early layers.
\end{proof}

We now show that an all-real analyticity ansatz cannot hold.  The obstruction
is a proved frozen half-line for the Fibonacci partition spectrum.  Let
\(\sigma_0>2\) be the unique real solution of
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\zeta(\sigma_0-1)}{\zeta(\sigma_0)}=2.
 \label{eq:freezing-exponent}
\end{equation}
Numerically, \(\sigma_0=2.478750785733960\ldots\).
The number \(-\sigma_0\) is initially obtained as a Dirichlet-series freezing
bound.  The weighted renewal argument in
\Cref{thm:exact-negative-pressure} subsequently proves that it is the exact
zero-pressure boundary.

\begin{theorem}[Negative-temperature freezing]
\label{thm:negative-temperature-freezing}
For every real \(t\le-\sigma_0\), the pressure exists unconditionally and
\[
 P(t):=\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac1m\log S_t(m)=0.
\]
Consequently there is no real-analytic function on \(\RR\) which agrees with
the finite-window pressure limits wherever those limits exist and also
satisfies \(P(0)=\log\varphi\).  In particular, no everywhere finite pressure
extension can be both real analytic and strictly convex on \(\RR\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For \(s>2\), put
\[
 A(s):=\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\frac{\phi_{\rm E}(n)}{n^s}
 =\frac{\zeta(s-1)}{\zeta(s)}-1,
\]
where \(\phi_{\rm E}\) is Euler's totient function.  Weinstein's free-monoid
decomposition and Tonelli's theorem give the following equality in
\([0,+\infty]\):
\begin{equation}
 1+\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\frac{\Psi(k)}{k^s}
 =\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}A(s)^r.
 \label{eq:weinstein-level-dirichlet-series}
\end{equation}
The geometric series is finite exactly when \(A(s)<1\), equivalently when
\(\zeta(s-1)/\zeta(s)<2\); in that region its value is
\((2-\zeta(s-1)/\zeta(s))^{-1}\).  The totient Dirichlet series also shows
that the ratio is strictly decreasing from \(+\infty\) to \(1\) on
\((2,\infty)\).  Hence \Cref{eq:freezing-exponent} has exactly one solution,
and \Cref{eq:weinstein-level-dirichlet-series} is finite precisely for
\(s>\sigma_0\).

By \Cref{thm:fiber-partition-interval}, the collision sum is exactly the sum
of \(R(n)^t\) over two adjacent Fibonacci layers:
\begin{equation}
 S_t(m)=\sum_{n=F_{m+1}-1}^{F_{m+3}-2}R(n)^t.
 \label{eq:real-collision-two-layers}
\end{equation}
If \(t<-\sigma_0\), the uniform layer-count lemma and
\Cref{eq:weinstein-level-dirichlet-series} give, uniformly in \(m\),
\[
 1\le S_t(m)
 \le 2+4\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\Psi(k)k^t<\infty.
\]
Thus \(m^{-1}\log S_t(m)\to0\).

At \(t=-\sigma_0\), fix \(\varepsilon>0\).  Only levels \(k\le D_m\)
occur, and for each such level
\[
 k^{-\sigma_0}\le D_m^\varepsilon k^{-(\sigma_0+\varepsilon)}.
\]
The same layer-count estimate therefore gives
\[
 S_{-\sigma_0}(m)
 \le 2+4D_m^{\varepsilon}
       \sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\frac{\Psi(k)}{k^{\sigma_0+\varepsilon}}.
\]
The series is finite, while
\(m^{-1}\log D_m\to\frac12\log\varphi\) by
\Cref{thm:unconditional-extremal-fibers}.  Therefore
\[
 0\le\limsup_{m\to\infty}\frac1m\log S_{-\sigma_0}(m)
 \le\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\log\varphi.
\]
Letting \(\varepsilon\downarrow0\) proves the endpoint assertion.

Finally, an analytic function which vanishes on the open interval
\(( -\infty,-\sigma_0)\) vanishes identically, whereas
\[
 P(0)=\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac1m\log|X_m|=\log\varphi.
\]
This proves the analytic and strict-convexity obstructions.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[No globally analytic compact-operator representation]
\label{prop:no-analytic-compact-representation}
There do not exist a complex Banach space \(\mathcal B\), a connected open
set \(U\subset\mathbb C\) containing \(\RR\), and an operator-norm analytic
map
\[
 z\longmapsto\mathcal L_z\in\mathcal K(\mathcal B)
 \qquad(z\in U)
\]
into the compact operators with the following properties.  For every real
\(t\), the operator \(\mathcal L_t\) has a positive algebraically simple
isolated eigenvalue \(r(t)\); for every \(t_0\in\RR\), this eigenvalue is
separated from the remainder of the spectrum by a positive gap uniformly for
\(t\) in some real neighborhood of \(t_0\); and the family represents the
finite-window sums in the exponential sense
\[
 \lim_{m\to\infty}\frac1m\log S_t(m)=\log r(t)
 \qquad(t\in\RR).
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For each \(m\ge1\), the function
\[
 p_m(t):=\frac1m\log S_t(m)
\]
is convex on \(\RR\).  Indeed, all fiber multiplicities are positive by the
interval-completeness lemma, and H\"older's inequality gives the defining
convexity inequality for \(S_t(m)\).  The representation hypothesis says that
\(p_m(t)\) converges pointwise on \(\RR\) to
\[
 P(t):=\log r(t).
\]
The finite pointwise limit \(P\) is therefore convex.  A finite convex function
on the open interval \(\RR\) is continuous, so \(r(t)=e^{P(t)}\) is continuous
on \(\RR\).

Fix \(t_0\in\RR\).  Choose a positively oriented contour \(\Gamma\) enclosing
\(r(t_0)\) and no other point of the spectrum of \(\mathcal L_{t_0}\).  For
complex \(z\) sufficiently close to \(t_0\), the contour remains in the
resolvent set and the Riesz projection
\[
 \Pi(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_\Gamma
  (\lambda I-\mathcal L_z)^{-1}\,d\lambda
\]
is analytic.  Its rank is locally constant and equals the algebraic
multiplicity of \(r(t_0)\), hence is one.  Analytic perturbation theory
\cite[Chap.~VII, Sec.~1]{Kato1995} therefore supplies a unique analytic
eigenvalue branch \(\mu(z)\) inside \(\Gamma\), with
\(\mu(t_0)=r(t_0)\).  The continuity of \(r(t)\) puts \(r(t)\) inside
\(\Gamma\) for all real \(t\) sufficiently close to \(t_0\); the local
spectral separation and rank-one Riesz projection then force
\(r(t)=\mu(t)\).  Thus \(r\), and hence \(t\mapsto\log r(t)\), is locally real
analytic at every \(t_0\).

The representation hypothesis makes this function equal to \(P(t)\).  By
\Cref{thm:negative-temperature-freezing}, it vanishes on
\(( -\infty,-\sigma_0)\), and therefore vanishes identically.  This
contradicts \(P(0)=\log\varphi\).
\end{proof}

The all-real renewal analysis in \Cref{thm:all-real-pressure} shows that the
pressure is real analytic and strictly convex on the positive-pressure phase,
but it also shows that the present freezing transition has a genuine corner:
the right derivative at \(-\sigma_0\) is strictly positive.  Thus the former
differentiability hypothesis at the transition is false, and the
G\"artner--Ellis theorem does not by itself provide lower bounds for slopes in
the interval between the two one-sided derivatives.  The finite-window
orbit-filling construction in \Cref{thm:full-fiber-ldp} provides precisely
these missing lower bounds and yields an unconditional full large deviation
principle.
